<hgroup><h1>Modeling and Validation of Anisotropic Thin-Film Deposition on Cylindrical Substrates for Predictable Resistance Control in MEMS Fabrication</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Tummala%20A"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Aditya Tummala</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Aditya Tummala</span></h3>
<div class="p">

<h3><span class="name western">Francesca Marturano</span></h3>
<div class="p">

<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>The complete version history of this preprint is available at <a href="https://doi.org/10.21203/rs.3.rs-8253144/v1" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Research Square</a>.</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ABS1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="P1">Precise control of electrical properties in conductive micro-structures is essential for the performance and reliability of micro-electro-mechanical systems (MEMS). However, the nature of anisotropic physical vapor deposition (PVD), such as electron-beam or thermal evaporation on curved or wire-like substrates, complicates the prediction of thin-film morphology and resulting electrical properties. This study develops and validates a geometrically explicit deposition model describing film growth on cylindrical substrates using a generalized pseudo-Lambertian cosine emission profile. Analytical expressions for local film thickness are derived as functions of deposition time, substrate geometry, and source collimation. Monte Carlo simulations confirm that the model accurately reproduces the deposition profile observed with simulated data <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M1" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn mathvariant="bold">2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn mathvariant="bold">0.99</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span>. A closed-form expression for resistance as a function of deposition parameters was also derived, integrating the Fuchs–Sondheimer and Mayadas–Shatzkes (FS-MS) frameworks to account for thin-film electron scattering and grain-boundary effects. Experimental validation was performed via electron-beam evaporation of gold onto cylindrical glass-core wires, with measured resistances spanning <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M2" display="inline"><mn mathvariant="bold">10</mn><mi mathvariant="bold">Ω</mi></math></span> to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M3" display="inline"><mn mathvariant="bold">1</mn><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">k</mi><mi mathvariant="bold">Ω</mi></math></span> across films <strong>70</strong> to <strong>3000</strong> nm thick. The FS-MS predicted resistances exhibited a Pearson correlation of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M4" display="inline"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">R</mi><mo>=</mo><mn mathvariant="bold">0.983</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">p</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn mathvariant="bold">0.001</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> with empirical measurements, confirming the model’s predictive accuracy. Additionally, this study develops an empirical mathematical model that captures the anisotropic behavior of PVD deposition on cylindrical surfaces. The model enables predictive control of thin-film resistivity in MEMS and bio-MEMS structures and offers a simulation framework that generalizes conventional planar thin-film modeling to complex, three-dimensional microfabrication topographies.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Physical Vapor Deposition, Gold Films, Fibers, Wires, Monte Carlo Simulation</p></section></section><section id="S1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p id="P2">Physical Vapor Deposition (PVD) systems play a crucial role in the fabrication of a wide range of electronic, optical, and micro-electro-mechanical systems (MEMS) applications, as well as in tool manufacturing and decorative coatings. In these systems, materials are vaporized and subsequently condensed onto a substrate, forming thin films with desirable properties such as electrical conductivity, wear resistance, or optical transparency. Depending on the technique, this vaporization may arise from resistive heating of a metal boat in thermal evaporators, a focused beam of excited electrons in electron beam (E-Beam) systems, or ion bombardment within a plasma during sputtering. Among PVD techniques, thermal evaporation and electron-beam deposition offer precise control over anisotropic material fluxes, enabling directional coating on complex geometries. When coupled with photolithography or etching, these methods facilitate the creation of micro- and nano-scale components integral to MEMS architectures, including micro-actuators, sensors, resonators, and biomedical interfaces.</p>
<p id="P3">Metallic and composite wires are among the most common substrates used in such applications, functioning as conductive pathways, micro-electrodes, or structural supports. For these components, achieving predictable electrical resistance is critical to ensuring device performance, sensitivity, and signal stability. However, resistance control in thin metallic films remains nontrivial due to the interplay between electron–matter interactions, surface scattering, and morphological variations inherent to the deposition process. In anisotropic PVD systems, deposition occurs primarily along line-of-sight trajectories, leaving shaded or occluded regions undercoated—a significant limitation when working with cylindrical or otherwise non-planar substrates typical in MEMS and bio-MEMS devices (See <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12776485_nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e4b1/12776485/d2e97c187dfe/nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="650" width="720" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P91">2-D cross section of a simulated deposition on a cylindrical substrate. Emission broadening exponent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M5" display="inline"><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></math></span>.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P4">Although recent advances in conformal deposition—such as high-pressure PVD (<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">Hass et al. (2004)</a>), hot-wire chemical vapor deposition (<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">Shi (2015)</a>; <a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">Schropp (2015)</a>), and atomic layer deposition (<a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">Yang et al. (2018)</a>)—have enhanced coating uniformity, these approaches often necessitate long and costly deposition times, elevated substrate temperatures or employ toxic and expensive precursors, restricting their suitability for bio-compatible MEMS applications. Consequently, despite the broad use of anisotropic PVD in micro-fabrication, existing models rarely capture the true spatial dependence of film growth on curved surfaces or the resulting electrical heterogeneity in deposited traces.</p>
<p id="P5">Classical frameworks, such as those proposed by <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Mayadas and Shatzkes (1970)</a> and <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Sondheimer (1952)</a>, relate film resistivity to micro-structural and scattering parameters; yet, they assume planar geometries and uniform thickness distributions. These simplifications often fail to reproduce empirical resistance measurements in wire-like systems, highlighting the need for a more comprehensive analytical treatment of anisotropic deposition processes.</p>
<p id="P6">This study addresses this gap by developing and validating a predictive framework for anisotropic thin-film deposition on cylindrical substrates. The Fuchs–Sondheimer (FS) and Mayadas–Shatzkes (MS) models (<a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Mayadas and Shatzkes (1970)</a>; <a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Fuchs (1938)</a>; <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Sondheimer (1952)</a>; <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>) are integrated with a geometrically resolved deposition model derived from first principles to account for boundary and scattering effects. Finally, this study presents an empirical mathematical model that describes the anisotropic deposition behavior of PVD films on cylindrical surfaces and establishes a foundation for predictive resistance control in MEMS and bio-MEMS fabrication, advancing the predictive design of conducting thin-film architectures. The predicted deposition profile is compared to three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulations, and the analytical resistance estimates are validated with experimental data from electron-beam deposited gold films.</p></section><section id="S2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. The Theory</h2>
<p id="P7">This section establishes a theoretical basis for modeling anisotropic material deposition during E-Beam evaporation onto cylindrical substrates. We first determine the geometric configuration governing the emission and incidence of vaporized particles on a generalizable cylindrical substrate to derive an analytical expression for the local film thickness distribution. This deposition profile is then used to formulate a predictive relationship between the deposition parameters and the resulting electrical resistance of the thin conductive trace.</p>
<section id="S3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. The Geometry</h3>
<p id="P8">The particle emission source is centered on the origin <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M25" display="inline"><mi>S</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>(</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span>. A cylindrical substrate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M26" display="inline"><mi>W</mi></math></span> of radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M27" display="inline"><mi>R</mi></math></span> about the z-axis is centered at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M28" display="inline"><mi>C</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>(</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M29" display="inline"><mi>D</mi></math></span> is defined as the distance between substrate center <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M30" display="inline"><mi>C</mi></math></span> and the emission source <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M31" display="inline"><mi>S</mi></math></span>. The cross-section in the x-y plane is shown in <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>. The bottom hemisphere of the circular cross–section of the cylinder is defined by
</p>

Deposition Profile</h3>
<p id="P14">According to this geometric setup, we can describe <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M66" display="inline"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>Φ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">emit</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> as the differential deposition rate at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M67" display="inline"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M68" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ℒ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mi>s</mi><msup><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> as the particle radiance where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M69" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> is defined as the number of particles emitted by the source. Then, as shown in <a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">Wang et al. (2018)</a>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M70" display="inline"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>Φ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">emit</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> can be expressed as
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M71" display="block"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>Φ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">emit</mtext></mrow></msub><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>ℒ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mrow><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mi>d</mi><mi>ω</mi><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>Φ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">emit</mtext></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P15">Substituting equation <a href="#FD8" class="usa-link">Eq. (8)</a> into <a href="#FD9" class="usa-link">Eq. (9)</a>, we attain:

<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P16">The local particle deposition density <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M73" display="inline"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is found by dividing <a href="#FD10" class="usa-link">Eq. (10)</a> by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M74" display="inline"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD11"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M75" display="block"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>ℒ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>ψ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cos</mtext><mo>(</mo><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>‖</mo><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mo>→</mo></mover><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><msup><mrow><mo>‖</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></math></td>

The rate of film growth <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M81" display="inline"><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mo>˙</mo></mover><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><mi>m</mi><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is given by multiplying <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M82" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> by the particle volume <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M83" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><msubsup><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></mrow></mfenced></math></span>. Given <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M84" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mn>0,1</mn><mo>]</mo></math></span> as the sticking probability, i.e., the probability that an incoming particle adheres to the previously deposited material, and the constant <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M85" display="inline"><mi>K</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> and substituting it into <a href="#FD12" class="usa-link">Eq. (12)</a>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD14"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M86" display="block"><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mo>˙</mo></mover><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>′</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mi>K</mi><mfrac><mrow><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>ψ</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cos</mtext><mo>(</mo><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>‖</mo><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mo>→</mo></mover><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><msup><mrow><mo>‖</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></math></td>

<p id="P18">Then, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M87" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is found by integration,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD15"><tr>

</tr></table>
<p id="P19">Substituting <a href="#FD6" class="usa-link">Eq. (6)</a> and <a href="#FD7" class="usa-link">Eq. (7)</a> into <a href="#FD15" class="usa-link">Eq. (14)</a>,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD16"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M89" display="block"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mfenced accent="true"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mo>→</mo></mover><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>‖</mo></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mo>‖</mo><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mo>→</mo></mover><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><msup><mrow><mo>‖</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></math></td>
<td class="label">(15)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P20">However, note that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M91" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mtext>lim</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>θ</mi><mo>→</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></msub><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></span>. Specifically, this occurs where the term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M92" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>D</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></span> (i.e. when <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M93" display="inline"><mtext>sin</mtext><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&gt;</mo><mo>-</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi></math></span>), that is, near the substrate boundaries. Indeed, for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M94" display="inline"><mi>θ</mi><mo>→</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></math></span> the raw analytic form of the model predicts small negative values near these edges because the cosine projection term allows for slight “overhang” where the surface normal begins to turn away from the source. However, in real physical vapor deposition, these regions simply receive negligible flux Since we assume <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M95" display="inline"><mi>D</mi><mo>≫</mo><mi>R</mi></math></span>, as is with almost all PVD systems, we can simplify the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M96" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>D</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> term to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M97" display="inline"><mi>D</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, which is always negative across <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M98" display="inline"><mi>θ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span>. The deposition at any point <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M99" display="inline"><mi>P</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> along the circumference of the cylindrical substrate (fixed <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M100" display="inline"><mi>R</mi></math></span>) at time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M101" display="inline"><mi>t</mi></math></span> is therefore given by:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD18"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M102" display="block"><mrow><menclose notation="box"><mrow><mi>f</mi><mfenced><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mo>−</mo><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>R</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mfenced><mrow><mi>D</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mfenced><mrow><msup><mi>R</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>D</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>R</mi><mi>D</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mn>2</mn></mfrac></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></menclose></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(16)</td>
</tr></table></section><section id="S5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Analytical Resistance Model</h3>
<p id="P21"><a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> provides an expression for evaluating the film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M103" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> deposited onto a cylindrical substrate surface as a function of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M104" display="inline"><mi>t</mi></math></span>, deposition geometry <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M105" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, and the emission broadening factor <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M106" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span>. We now extend this model to analytically determine the resulting electrical resistance of the deposited metallic layer, accounting for non-uniform thickness, curvature effects, and thin-film boundary scattering phenomena. To accurately capture electron transport within the thin conductive layer, we employ the Fuchs–Sondheimer (FS) and Mayadas–Shatzkes (MS) frameworks (<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Fuchs (1938)</a>; <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>), which correct for grain-boundary and surface-scattering effects that dominate at nanometric scales. During PVD, nanometer-scale film accumulation always occurs at the edges of cylindrical substrates.</p>
<p id="P22">At a time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M107" display="inline"><mi>t</mi></math></span>, substrate radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M108" display="inline"><mi>R</mi></math></span>, and angle <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M109" display="inline"><mi>θ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span>, the thickness of deposition is given by <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>. To evaluate the total conductive cross-section formed by the deposited material <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M110" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span>, <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> is integrated over <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M111" display="inline"><mi>θ</mi></math></span> from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M112" display="inline"><mi>π</mi><mo>→</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></math></span>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD19"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M113" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="true">∫</mo><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mi>f</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mi>R</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>θ</mi><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo>]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(17)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P23">For typical deposition conditions, the distance from the source to the substrate is significantly larger than the substrate radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M114" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>≪</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>; thus, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M115" display="inline"><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>≪</mo><mn>1</mn></math></span>. Substituting <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> into <a href="#FD19" class="usa-link">Eq. (17)</a>, setting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M116" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mi>D</mi></math></span>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD20"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M117" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>-</mo><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="true">∫</mo><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mfrac><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mspace separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>ϵ</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mi>R</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><msup><mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>ϵ</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></msup><mi>d</mi><mi>θ</mi></math></td>
<td class="label">(18)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P24">Expanding to first order in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M119" display="inline"><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></span>, dropping <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M120" display="inline"><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span>, by the binomial theorem, we have that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M121" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>x</mi><msup><mrow><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow></msup><mo>≃</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>a</mi><mi>x</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced><mo>+</mo><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced><mo>+</mo><mo>…</mo></math></span>. Applying this to <a href="#FD20" class="usa-link">Eq. (18)</a>, we expand each term in the integrand:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD22"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M122" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="true">∫</mo><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>n</mi><mi>ϵ</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mo>-</mo><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>ϵ</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>θ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow><mi>θ</mi></math></td>
<td class="label">(19)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P25">Substituting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M123" display="inline"><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></span> in <a href="#FD22" class="usa-link">Eq. (19)</a>, results in the final expression<sup><a href="#FN2" class="usa-link">2</a></sup> for the cross-sectional area of deposition <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M124" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD23"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M125" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>≈</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></td>
<td class="label">(20)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P26">The first term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M126" display="inline"><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is dominant, the second term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M127" display="inline"><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><msup><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is a small correction <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M128" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mo>∝</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>D</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>)</mo></math></span>, and the rest is negligible <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M129" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>≪</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P27">The cross-sectional area of deposition <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M130" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, can be used to calculate the predicted resistance of the cylindrical substrate after time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M131" display="inline"><mi>t</mi></math></span> seconds of deposition and a constant volumetric “flow-rate” of material <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M132" display="inline"><mi>K</mi><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><msup><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span>. The trace resistance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M133" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">tot</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>Ω</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is given by:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD24"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M134" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></td>
<td class="label">(21)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M135" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">tot</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> is resistance, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M136" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>Ω</mi><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is the resistivity of deposited material, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M137" display="inline"><mi>L</mi><mo>[</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is the length of the substrate, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M138" display="inline"><mi>A</mi><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is the cross-sectional area of the substrate.</p>
<p id="P28">The resistivity ratio, using the correction for thin metal layers based on the Fuchs-Sondheimer (FS) and Mayadas-Shatzkes (MS) models (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>) to account for boundary and scattering effects in thin films, is given by:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD25"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M139" display="block"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>eff</mtext></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo>≃</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac><mi>α</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>log</mtext><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>8</mn><mi>k</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></td>
<td class="label">(22)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M140" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>eff</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>Ω</mi><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> depends on the thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M141" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>Ω</mi><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is the bulk resistivity of Au, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M142" display="inline"><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><mi>m</mi><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> (unitless), and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M143" display="inline"><mi>P</mi></math></span> is the fraction of electrons specularly scattered at the external surfaces (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>; <a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">Mayadas (1968)</a>; <a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">Tellier and Tosser (1977)</a>). Additionally, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M144" display="inline"><mi>α</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfrac><mo>[</mo><mi>Ω</mi><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> whereby <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M145" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is the mean free path of electrons, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M146" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is the grain boundary reflection constant, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M147" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub><mo>[</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>]</mo></math></span> is average material crystalline diameter.</p>
<p id="P29">Letting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M148" display="inline"><mi>F</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>≔</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac><mi>α</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>log</mtext><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>α</mi></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></math></span>, the FS-MS model becomes:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD26"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M149" display="block"><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>eff</mtext></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo>≅</mo><mi>F</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>8</mn><mi>k</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mi>F</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>-</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>8</mn></mrow></mfrac><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfrac></math></td>
<td class="label">(23)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P30">The longitudinal conductance per unit length <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M150" display="inline"><mi>G</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> as the parallel sum over the cross-section of the cylindrical substrate<sup><a href="#FN3" class="usa-link">3</a></sup> is:
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD27"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M151" display="block"><mi>G</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo stretchy="true">∫</mo><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></msubsup><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>f</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mi>f</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mfenced><mi>R</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo></math></td>
<td class="label">(24)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where the effective conductance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M152" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math></span>, and thus by <a href="#FD26" class="usa-link">Eq. (23)</a>,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD28"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M153" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>σ</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>f</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>)</mo><mo>≃</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>8</mn></mrow></mfrac><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfrac></math></td>
<td class="label">(25)</td>
</tr></table>
<p id="P31">Substituting <a href="#FD28" class="usa-link">Eq. (25)</a> into <a href="#FD27" class="usa-link">Eq. (24)</a> to find <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M154" display="inline"><mi>G</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>,

<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD29"><tr>

(2023)</a>). Incorporating <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M160" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> into <a href="#FD29" class="usa-link">Eq. (26)</a> and simplifying, the resistance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M161" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> on a generalizable cylindrical substrate is
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD31"><tr>

where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M163" separators="|"><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced id="S6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Methods</h2>
<section id="S7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Monte Carlo Simulations</h3>
<p id="P33">The deposition profile predicted by <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> was studied using a three-dimensional Monte Carlo simulation developed in Python 3.10 (Google Colab IDE environment) to model particle trajectories originating from a point-approximated evaporation source exhibiting a prototypical pseudo-Lambertian emission profile<sup><a href="#FN4" class="usa-link">4</a></sup>
<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M165" display="inline"><mfenced close="" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><mo>-</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac><mo>,</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></math></span>). The deposition substrate was represented as a cylindrical wire of 2 cm length <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M166" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M167" display="inline"><mn>50</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mtext>m</mtext></math></span> radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M168" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, with center <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M169" display="inline"><mi>C</mi></math></span> positioned coaxially above the source. The source-to-substrate throw distance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M170" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>—a key parameter influencing deposition rate and uniformity—varies widely in literature depending on desired film properties (<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Yang et al. (2001b)</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Venkattraman and Alexeenko (2011)</a>; <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>). In this simulation, a throw distance of 5.0 cm was selected, following the optimized configuration reported by <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>, to maximize film uniformity and minimize resistive variability along the cylindrical surface<sup><a href="#FN5" class="usa-link">5</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P34">A total of 5 billion macro-particles were emitted from the source, of which 1,851,741 were successfully deposited onto the substrate. Each macro-particle corresponded to the deposition of 6.09 × 10<sup>9</sup> gold atoms, resulting in a maximum film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M171" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>max</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>≈</mo><mn>100</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>nm</mtext></math></span>. This was calculated as an average deposition thickness of the immediate neighboring bins at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M172" display="inline"><mi>P</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>θ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mi>R</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mrow><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>R</mi><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>sin</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>+</mo><mi>D</mi></mrow></mfenced></math></span> at time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M173" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">max</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> (region of maximal deposition directly above the source). The simulation took 20 minutes to run (see Supplementary Materials for additional simulation parameters and open-source code). The computed <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M174" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>max</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span><sup><a href="#FN6" class="usa-link">6</a></sup> was determined independently of the simulation bin size, as the raw hit counts were normalized to flux per unit area before thickness conversion. Parameter <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M175" display="inline"><mi>K</mi><mi>t</mi><mfenced open="[" close="]" separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span> was solved for by setting <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M176" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">max</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mn>100</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>nm</mtext></math></span> in <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>.</p></section><section id="S8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Empiric Resistance Validation Setup</h3>
<p id="P35">To validate the analytical model for substrate resistance described by <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>, empirical measurements were conducted on E-Beam evaporated gold films deposited onto cylindrical substrates of various diameters and lengths under controlled vacuum conditions. These experiments aimed to directly compare the predicted resistance values reported by <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a> with resistances measured post-deposition using a two-point ohmmeter and impedance spectroscopy methods.</p>
<p id="P36">Gold (Au) was selected for its well-characterized bulk resistivity and widespread use in MEMS manufacturing and circuitry. Cylindrical substrates consisted of glass-core wires (radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M177" display="inline"><mn>55</mn><mo>-</mo><mn>300</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mtext>m</mtext></math></span>, length varying from 1.5 to 43.0 cm), mounted on a substrate holder. The source-to-substrate distance was maintained at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M178" display="inline"><mi>D</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>20.0</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cm</mtext></math></span>, and deposition was performed under a base pressure between 2 × 10<sup>−6</sup> and 5.0 × 10<sup>−7</sup> Torr with ambient deposition temperature <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M179" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mi>m</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M180" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mn>20</mn></mrow><mrow><mo>∘</mo></mrow></msup><mi>C</mi></math></span>. Deposition rates, monitored via piezoelectric quartz crystal microbalance, ranged between 1.0 and 3.0 <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M181" display="inline"><mo>Å</mo><mo>/</mo><mi>s</mi></math></span>, with film thicknesses <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M182" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">max</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> between 70 and 3000 nm (PVD deposition setpoint).</p>
<p id="P37">An empirical estimate, referred to as the Empiric Model, was also developed by performing a least-squares regression on the experimental data to determine an empiric scaling constant, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M183" display="inline"><mi>𝒞</mi></math></span>, that optimally scales the conventional formula for trace resistance <a href="#FD24" class="usa-link">Eq. (21)</a>,
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD32"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M184" display="block"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>𝒞</mi><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></mfrac><msub><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>𝒞</mi><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>W</mi></mrow></mfrac><msub><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></math></td>
<td class="label">(28)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M185" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> is the cylindrical substrate resistance, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M186" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span> is the bulk resistivity of Au, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M187" display="inline"><mi>L</mi></math></span> is the cylindrical substrate length, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M188" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>a</mi><mi>d</mi><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> is the temperature adjustment coefficient (defined above in <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M189" display="inline"><mi>T</mi></math></span> is the deposition thickness, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M190" display="inline"><mi>W</mi></math></span> is the deposition trace width, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M191" display="inline"><mi>𝒞</mi></math></span> is the empiric constant that optimally scales the conventional trace resistance formula expressed by <a href="#FD24" class="usa-link">Eq. (21)</a>. Empirically, this represents the “width” of deposition on the wire substrate, as the trace width <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M192" display="inline"><mi>W</mi></math></span> is scaled by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M193" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mi>𝒞</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math></span>. This is done to account for the empiric uncertainty on the trace width of deposition on the substrate, as <a href="#FD32" class="usa-link">Eq. (28)</a> assumes uniform deposition thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M194" display="inline"><mi>T</mi></math></span> across the entirety of the trace width <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M195" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mi>𝒞</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>W</mi></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P38">Theoretical predictions were computed using the parameters extracted from the deposition geometry <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M196" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>K</mi><mo>,</mo><mtext>and</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> and material constants <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M197" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mtext>and</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>P</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> taken from literature (see <a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>

</div></section><p id="P39">The resistance values measured across <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M198" display="inline"><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>20</mn></math></span> wires were compared to those predicted by the FS-MS adjusted model described by <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a> as well as those predicted by the Empiric Model in <a href="#FD32" class="usa-link">Eq. (28)</a>. To quantitatively assess the agreement between the analytical FS–MS model predictions, the empirical model predictions, and experimental resistance data, a statistical correlation and least-squares regression analysis were performed with outliers removed.</p></section></section><section id="S9"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Results</h2>
<section id="S10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Monte Carlo Deposition Profile Validation</h3>
<p id="P40">Results of the Monte Carlo Simulation are shown in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>. The Monte Carlo simulated deposition thickness profile on a cylindrical substrate was compared to the theoretical deposition profile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M199" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> predicted by the E-Beam evaporation model <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>. Results of this validation are shown in <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>. Final fitting (without Gaussian smoothing) validated the alignment of the theoretical deposition profile to the simulated profile, confirming that the geometric model captures the essential shape and falloff of the measured film thickness around the cylinder. The root mean square error (RMSE) was found to be 3.56418 nm, the mean absolute error (MAE) was 3.22174 nm, and coefficient of determination <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M200" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>)</mo></math></span> was 0.985204.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12776485_nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e4b1/12776485/43bcc4f5def7/nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="616" width="750" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>

The shaded represents ±2 standard deviations for simulation data.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.2. Empiric Deposition Profile Validation</h3>
<p id="P41">Cross-sections of the manufactured cylindrical substrates were prepared using a JEOL Broad Ion Beam Milling Cross Section Polisher and imaged using a Zeiss Gemini 560 Field Emission Scanning Electron Microscope (See <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12776485_nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e4b1/12776485/d778a946a27f/nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="563" width="750" alt="Fig. 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P97">SEM image of substrate cross-section radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M8" display="inline"><mn>250</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mtext>m</mtext></math></span> with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M9" display="inline"><mn>1</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mtext>m</mtext></math></span> Au deposited through electron-beam PVD</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.3. Empiric Resistance Validation</h3>
<p id="P42">Results of model comparisons to empirically measured resistance values can be found in <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, reporting Pearson correlation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M201" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></math></span>, RMSE, and MAE. For the Empiric Model, least squares fitting of <a href="#FD32" class="usa-link">Eq. (28)</a> to empiric data yielded <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M202" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mi>𝒞</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>W</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.172</mn><mo>(</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, representing an empirical “deposition trace width” of ≈ 17.2% of the total circumference <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M203" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> of the cylindrical substrate. Both models indicate strong linear relationships with the measured data, confirming that they accurately capture the overall trend of resistance across a range of varying deposition parameters (See <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>).</p>
