<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F8"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12776485_nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e4b1/12776485/3e19eb85a78c/nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="472" width="720" alt="Fig. 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P98">Comparison of measured sample resistance, Empiric Model predictions, and FS-MS Model predictions. 95% CI is plotted for samples with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M10" display="inline"><mi>n</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>1</mn></math></span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="S13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">5. Discussion</h2>
<p id="P43">In this study, we derive, validate, and present both a mathematical model and a simplified empirical model to predict electrical properties of anisotropic conductive traces on cylindrical substrates. The derived geometric deposition profile (<a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>) and the FS-MS electron transport-corrected resistance model (<a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>) jointly explain the behavior of anisotropic PVD on cylindrical substrates, and where measured data deviate from FS-MS and scaled trace model predictions. Monte Carlo simulations were performed to validate that a pseudo-Lambertian plume <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M204" display="inline"><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></mrow></mfenced></math></span> coupled to line-of-sight geometry reproduces the measured angular thickness distribution with high fidelity, verifying <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>. A non-uniform thickness profile <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M205" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> was mapped onto a parallel-channel conductance integral and incorporating FS–MS scattering corrections to yield resistance predictions. These model predictions were compared to experimental results across 70–3000 nm films. Further, this experimental data was employed to benchmark a simplified empiric trace approximation model, which captures the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M206" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∝</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>max</mtext></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup></math></span> relationship with empiric scaling term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M207" display="inline"><mi>𝒞</mi></math></span>, but misses curvature-induced anisotropy and thin-film scattering, clarifying when rapid estimates suffice and when full FS–MS corrections are necessary. Finally, the emission-shape parameter <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M208" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> and the substrate radius to throw distance ratio <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M209" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi></math></span> were interpreted as practical design knobs for targeting resistance, and outline how additional considerations—angle-dependent sticking probability <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M210" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub><mo>∈</mo><mo>[</mo><mn>0,1</mn><mo>]</mo></math></span>, multilayer stacks, and rotation/planetary fixtures—can serve to narrow the remaining gap between theory and fabrication realities relevant to MEMS and bio-MEMS applications.</p>
<section id="S14"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.1. Principal Findings</h3>
<p id="P44">This study presents three key findings with significant implications for MEMS, bio-MEMS, and also non-MEMS electrical component design and fabrication. First, the analytical deposition model derived in <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> successfully reproduces the spatio-temporal dependence of film thickness on cylindrical substrates under anisotropic PVD. Monte Carlo simulations incorporating 5 × 10<sup>9</sup> particle trajectories confirmed a close agreement with the predicted deposition distribution <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M211" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0.985</mn><mo>,</mo><mtext>RMSE</mtext><mo>=</mo><mn>3.56</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>nm</mtext><mo>)</mo></math></span>, demonstrating that the pseudo-Lambertian emission assumption <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M212" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M213" display="inline"><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> and geometric formulation capture the essential angular dependence of the deposited flux. Second, by integrating the Fuchs–Sondheimer and Mayadas–Shatzkes (FS–MS) electron-scattering frameworks with this derived geometric model, an electron transport-corrected resistance expression (<a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>) was developed. This formulation quantitatively links deposition parameters of time <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M214" display="inline"><mi>t</mi></math></span>, throw distance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M215" display="inline"><mi>D</mi></math></span>, and emission collimation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M216" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> to effective resistivity and total trace resistance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M217" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>. Comparison with experimentally measured resistances across gold films ranging from 70 to 3000 nm in thickness yielded a strong correlation (Pearson <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M218" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.985,0.983</mn></math></span> for the Empiric Model and FS-MS Model, respectively), validating the models’ predictive accuracy for non-planar thin-film systems. Finally, a simplified empirical trace approximation model based on the classic relation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M219" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>∝</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>/</mo><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>max</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> was fitted via least-squares regression to experimental data. While this reduced model captures the general scaling trend, it systematically underestimates the resistance of thinner films due to its neglect of curvature, anisotropic coverage, and surface-scattering effects, resulting in greater error as film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M220" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> approaches either 0 or <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M221" display="inline"><mi>∞</mi></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P45">Together, these results confirm that the combined geometric and FS–MS transport framework provides a physically consistent and experimentally validated model for predicting electrical resistance in anisotropically deposited metallic coatings on cylindrical substrates, offering a foundation for more precise resistance control in MEMS and bio-MEMS fabrication.</p></section><section id="S15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.2. Physical Considerations</h3>
<section id="S16"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.2.1. Anisotropic Plume Parameter <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M2220" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span>
</h4>
<p id="P46">The emission exponent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M222" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> in the generalized pseudo-Lambertian term <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M223" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> governs the angular distribution of vapor flux emitted from the source surface (<a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Yang et al. (2001a)</a>; <a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Reinhold et al. (2000)</a>). Physically, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M224" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> quantifies the collimation or “sharpness” of the emission plume. As portrayed by <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>, smaller values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M225" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>≈</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> correspond to uniform, diffuse emission of particles from the source. In other words, a Lambertian emission profile has equal particle radiance from the source regardless of the viewing angle. Lambertian emission <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M226" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> is typical of emission from a Knudsen Cell emitter, often used for conformal coating of the interior surface of hollow spherical substrates such as light bulbs and for planetary wafer tooling in vacuum coating equipment (<a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">Darling (n.d.)</a>). Pseudo-Lambertian emission <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M227" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> is typical of thermal evaporation or simpler electron-beam sources, whereas larger values <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M228" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> describe increasingly forward-directed, collimated plumes that can arise from more directed electron-beam or ion-assisted sources with limited scattering of vaporized particles in the gas phase (<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Reinhold et al. (2000)</a>; <a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">Yang et al. (2001a)</a>). In experimental systems, the effective <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M229" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> is influenced by the source-to-substrate throw distance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M230" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, chamber pressure, and the kinetic energy of the evaporated species, all of which alter the degree of angular broadening observed at the substrate (<a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">Powell et al. (2000)</a>; <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Iriarte et al. (2011)</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h5 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12776485_nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e4b1/12776485/47fece766820/nihpp-rs8253144v1-f0004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="323" width="720" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P94">Source emission plume for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M7" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, modeling various values for n</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P47">In the derived model for deposition morphology <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M231" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> directly modulates the falloff of the deposited film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M232" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> away from the point of normal incidence <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M233" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfrac><mo>)</mo></math></span>. Increasing <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M234" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> therefore steepens the angular roll-off and reduces deposition on the lateral and occluded surfaces of the cylinder, effectively narrowing the emission of conductive material onto the substrate. For small substrate lengths, this has little effect on the longitudinal deposition thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M235" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> in the x-direction. However, as the distance from substrate to source becomes smaller or as substrate length becomes longer, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M236" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> has an increasingly significant effect on overall deposition uniformity and consequent electrical properties.</p>
<p id="P48">In practical applications, the effective value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M237" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> can be estimated empirically by fitting measured thickness profiles or deposition rates to the analytical form of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M238" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>. For a given material and chamber configuration, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M239" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> can be calibrated through a simple reference deposition onto a planar witness sample positioned at multiple off-axis angles, as outlined in <a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Fuchs (1938)</a> and <a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">Sohn (2019)</a>. Once determined, the calibrated <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M240" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> serves as a transferable system parameter for predictive modeling of coating uniformity on curved or wire-like substrates, providing a key input for resistance tuning and process optimization in MEMS fabrication.</p></section><section id="S17"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.2.2. FS–MS Corrections</h4>
<p id="P49">The Fuchs–Sondheimer (FS) and Mayadas–Shatzkes (MS) models are utilized in the derived model for resistance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M241" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> (<a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>). Together, these models provide the physical foundation for understanding how thin metallic films deviate from bulk resistivity due to boundary and grain-boundary electron scattering effects (<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Fuchs (1938)</a>; <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Mayadas and Shatzkes (1970)</a>; <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>). In bulk conductors, electrons undergo primarily diffusive scattering, and resistivity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M242" display="inline"><mi>ρ</mi></math></span> remains constant with respect to film thickness. However, when the film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M243" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> approaches or falls below the electron mean free path <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M244" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span>, surface and grain-boundary scattering begin to significantly impede carrier motion, causing an increase in effective resistivity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M245" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">eff</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> relative to the bulk value <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M246" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span> (<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">Fuchs (1938)</a>; <a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">Sondheimer (1952)</a>; <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">Mayadas and Shatzkes (1970)</a>; <a href="#R36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R36">Tellier and Tosser (1977)</a>). The FS model accounts for electron reflection at the film surfaces by introducing a specular scattering fraction <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M247" display="inline"><mi>P</mi></math></span>, representing the probability that an electron reflects specularly rather than diffusely, while the MS formulation extends this correction to include reflection from grain boundaries parameterized by the reflection coefficient <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M248" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> and the mean crystalline grain size <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M249" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>. Both of these effects are incorporated in the FS–MS approximation used in this study (<a href="#FD26" class="usa-link">Eq. (23)</a>), yielding a continuous relationship between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M250" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext>eff</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M251" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P50">The influence of FS–MS corrections diminishes as film thickness increases beyond several times <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M252" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span>, at which point electron scattering becomes dominated by phonon (representations of quantized lattice vibration) interactions rather than boundary effects (<a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">Lacy (2011)</a>). However, in micro-fabrication contexts where thin conductive traces are intentionally deposited at low thicknesses to achieve specific resistances or minimize parasitic coupling, surface and grain-boundary scattering dominate the transport behavior (<a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">Islam et al. (2024)</a>; <a href="#R35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R35">Sun et al. (2009)</a>). The strong experimental correlation between measured and FS–MS–predicted resistances (Pearson <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M253" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.983</mn><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0.967</mn></math></span>) observed here confirms that these corrections are essential for accurate modeling of non-planar, anisotropic PVD coatings (See <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a> and <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>).</p>
<p id="P51">Finally, the FS–MS framework also provides an interpretive bridge between morphological observations and electrical performance. SEM imaging (See <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>) reveals columnar grain structures and interfacial voids consistent with partial electron reflection <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M254" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.25</mn><mo>-</mo><mn>0.4</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> and low specular scattering <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M255" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.1</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span>, values aligned with prior measurements of polycrystalline gold films (<a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Zhang et al. (2011)</a>; <a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">Kästle et al. (2004)</a>). The close quantitative match between these independently derived parameters and the fitted model coefficients underscores the physical robustness of the FS–MS–adjusted resistance formulation, validating its applicability for thin-film systems exhibiting anisotropic deposition, curvature, and micro-structural heterogeneity.</p></section><section id="S18"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.2.3. Sources of Discrepancy Between Model and Experiment</h4>
<p id="P52">While the FS–MS–corrected analytical model (<a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>) reproduces the overall resistance trends with high fidelity (Pearson <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M256" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.983</mn><mo>,</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>0.967</mn></math></span>), several systematic deviations between predicted and measured values arise due to additional physical and experimental effects not explicitly included in the present formulation. These discrepancies originate primarily from (1) microstructural evolution during early-stage film growth, (2) mechanical degradation of the deposited layer (i.e., substrate damage and defects), (3) electrical contact variability during measurement, and (4) morphological and geometric effects not captured in the idealized model assumptions.</p>
<p id="P53"><strong>(1) Island formation and percolation thresholds.</strong> In the initial stages of PVD film growth, as described by the Volmer-Weber growth model, gold atoms nucleate into discrete surface islands that coalesce as thickness increases (<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">Volmer and Weber (1926)</a>). Before complete coalescence, these discontinuous films exhibit significantly higher resistivity due to limited percolation pathways and quantum-size effects (<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">Mirigliano et al. (2020)</a>; <a href="#R3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R3">Cho et al. (2025)</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">Morris (2022)</a>). Other models, such as Stranski–Krastanov growth, include the consideration of island formation, while blending theories of conformal deposition and percolation, such as that formalized later on by the Frank-Van der Merwe model of layer-by-layer growth (<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">Stranski and Krastanov (1938)</a>; <a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">Frank and der Merwe (1949)</a>). The analytical model derived in this work assumes a continuous “layer-by-layer” model of deposition, whereby there exists a conductive layer from the onset of deposition and thus underestimates resistance for films below ~100 nm. This explains the slight upward deviation of experimental resistance from theoretical predictions in the thinnest films (See <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>).</p>
<p id="P54"><strong>(2) Mechanical cracking and flaking.</strong> SEM imaging (See <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>) and post-handling observations revealed local delamination and micro-crack formation along the curved substrate surface. These phenomena arise from tensile stress gradients across the wire substrate and poor adhesion at the gold-glass interface, especially at oblique incidence angles where film density and thickness are reduced, despite a thin 10 nm Ti layer used for adhesion. Such discontinuities interrupt conductive pathways, increasing measured resistance beyond the FS–MS prediction, which assumes a uniform, consistently-adherent film. Similar flaking and cracking-induced resistance jumps have been reported in evaporated thin films of gold and various other common PVD materials on fiber and polymer substrates (<a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">Avilés et al. (2009)</a>; <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">Mironov et al. (2024)</a>; <a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Panjan et al. (2020)</a>).</p>
<p id="P55"><strong>(3) Contact and measurement artifacts.</strong> The resistance measurements were performed primarily using a two-point ohmmeter probe, which introduces small but systematic contact resistances at the wire–electrode interfaces. Although minimized by repeated averaging, these effects become proportionally significant in low-resistance samples <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M257" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>10</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>Ω</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>. Small variations in probe placement, contamination at the gold surface, and probe pressure can further increase apparent resistance, particularly for short-length substrates. Fabrication with robust contacts or Kelvin sensing could mitigate this uncertainty in future work.</p>
<p id="P56"><strong>(4) Geometric simplifications and unmodeled effects.</strong> As discussed in <a href="#S2" class="usa-link">Section 2</a>, the theoretical model treats the evaporation source as a perfect point emitter and neglects secondary scattering in the vapor phase, assuming entirely free molecular flow. In practice, however, non-uniform angular flux and evaporation crucible or boat source area produce subtle shifts in the peak deposition angle and broaden the film distribution compared to the idealized <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M258" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> profile. Additionally, substrate curvature, rotation, micro/nano defects, and surface roughness can locally modify the incidence angle <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M259" display="inline"><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> and sticking probability <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M260" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, introducing small spatial variations in film thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M261" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> not captured analytically. These combined geometric effects yield slight asymmetries in the measured film thickness around the cylindrical substrate and contribute to residual variance in the experimental data (<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Yang et al. (2001b)</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Venkattraman and Alexeenko (2011)</a>; <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>).</p>
<p id="P57">Collectively, these effects highlight the major considerations in assuming perfect continuity, adhesion, and isotropic electron transport in thin metallic coatings. Nonetheless, the residual error between the model and experiment remains well within experimental uncertainty, confirming that the dominant behavior of anisotropic PVD on cylindrical substrates is accurately described by the combined geometric and FS–MS theoretical framework.</p></section></section><section id="S19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.3. Experimental Constraints and Approach</h3>
<p id="P58">While the theoretical expressions of <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> and <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a> assume idealized boundary conditions, their application to real-world PVD systems necessitates controlled simplifications to achieve both computational tractability and experimental reproducibility. The following subsections outline the key methodological considerations, parameter sensitivities, and experimental constraints that shaped the design, validation, and interpretation of results.</p>
<section id="S20"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.3.1. Monte Carlo Methodological Remarks</h4>
<p id="P59">The Monte Carlo simulation framework developed for this study was designed to isolate purely geometric and angular effects in anisotropic deposition, independent of material-specific phenomena such as re-emission, surface diffusion, or temperature and incident angle-dependent sticking probability. Each emitted macro-particle was assigned an initial direction drawn from the normalized pseudo-Lambertian distribution <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M262" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M263" display="inline"><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></math></span>. This configuration reflects the typical emission profile of E-Beam evaporation sources operating under high vacuum (&lt; 10<sup>−6</sup> Torr), where we assume free molecular flow and gas-phase scattering is negligible (<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Yang et al. (2001b)</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Venkattraman and Alexeenko (2011)</a>).</p>
<p id="P60">A total of 5 × 10<sup>9</sup> particle trajectories were simulated to ensure a statistical convergence of the deposited flux profile on the cylindrical substrate. Rather than interpreting raw hit counts in each surface bin directly as thickness, the tally in each bin was converted to a flux density by normalizing each bin with respect to both the local deposition area and the corresponding solid angle subtended at the source. In other words, each bin recorded particles per unit area per unit solid angle, so that changes in the number or size of bins do not alter the recovered angular thickness profile. This area and solid-angle normalization prevent spatial discretization artifacts that would otherwise arise from uneven bin sizes or coarse angular sampling, ensuring that the simulated thickness distribution remains invariant under mesh refinement. These bin-independent flux estimates are essential for making meaningful, bin resolution-agnostic comparisons between the Monte Carlo results and the continuous analytic solution for deposition thickness <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M264" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>.</p>
<p id="P61">The chosen throw distance of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M265" display="inline"><mi>D</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>5.0</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cm</mtext></math></span> and cylinder radius of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M266" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>50</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mtext>m</mtext></math></span> correspond to an optimal practical geometry used in small-scale MEMS tooling and fiber metallization systems (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>; <a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">Yang et al. (2001b)</a>; <a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">Venkattraman and Alexeenko (2011)</a>). This radius-to-distance ratio <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M267" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.001</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> ensures that line-of-sight deposition dominates, consistent with the small-angle approximation used in deriving <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a>. The Monte Carlo model thus serves primarily to validate the angular dependence and geometric fidelity of the analytical framework rather than to replicate detailed atomic transport or post-deposition grain percolation. Future refinements may incorporate rotational motion, substrate heating, or diffusive re-emission effects to further approximate the physical PVD environment.</p></section><section id="S21"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.3.2. Experimental Constraints and Imaging Challenges</h4>
<p id="P62">Empirical validation of thin-film thickness and morphology on cylindrical substrates presents several technical challenges that inherently limit the precision of comparison between model and experiment beyond simple resistance comparison. Unlike planar substrates, wires or fiber-like samples are difficult to cross-section cleanly without introducing mechanical deformation or fracture. Indeed, even minor cutting artifacts can distort the apparent thickness or cause delamination at the metal–substrate interface. To minimize these effects, cross-sections were prepared using broad ion-beam (BIB) milling and imaged via field-emission scanning electron microscopy (FESEM), providing near-vertical cuts (See <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). Nevertheless, curvature-induced charging and sample tilt occasionally limited image clarity in the peripheral regions, particularly at large <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M268" display="inline"><mi>θ</mi></math></span>. Additionally, the ratio of deposition thickness to substrate radius <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M269" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">max</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>/</mo><mi>R</mi></math></span> is very small. Both achieving large thicknesses and using extremely fine substrates <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M270" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>10</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mi>μ</mi><mi>m</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> are resource-intensive, time-intensive, and overall highly impractical, especially with gold films. Therefore, visual measurements of the cross-section to characterize the deposition profile tend to be extremely inconsistent and non-viable.</p>
<p id="P63">Despite these challenges, however, the combination of analytical modeling, high-fidelity simulation, electrical measurements, and careful imaging provided sufficient quantitative agreement to validate the theoretical framework. The results demonstrate that even under non-ideal experimental conditions, the anisotropic deposition behavior and its impact on electrical performance can be accurately captured through geometric modeling, properly calibrated FS–MS corrections, and controlled measurement methodology.</p></section></section><section id="S22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.4. Applications</h3>
<p id="P64">Beyond validating the physical accuracy of the analytical and FS–MS–corrected model frameworks, the results of this study have direct implications for the design and optimization of conductive thin-film structures in microscale and biomedical device fabrication (MEMS and bio-MEMS), as well as other micro-electronic use cases. The developed models establish a set of scalable, analytical tools that connect geometric deposition parameters to measurable electrical outcomes, thereby enabling predictive control of resistance and film morphology across diverse PVD configurations.</p>
<section id="S23"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.4.1. Model Applicability</h4>
<p id="P65">The proposed model is broadly applicable to anisotropic PVD environments in which film growth is dominated by line-of-sight vapor flux and negligible scattering in the vapor phase (assumption of free molecular flow). This includes PVD processes like electron-beam evaporation, thermal evaporation, and certain ion-beam-assisted deposition (IBAD) systems under high vacuum (&lt; 10<sup>−6</sup> Torr) (<a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">Mattox (2010)</a>; <a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Panjan et al. (2020)</a>). The geometric foundation expressed in <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> remains valid as long as the substrate curvature satisfies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M271" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>≪</mo><mn>1</mn></math></span>, ensuring that occlusion and self-shadowing effects can be modeled through the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M272" display="inline"><msup><mrow><mtext>cos</mtext></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>ψ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M273" display="inline"><mtext>cos</mtext><mo>(</mo><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> angular factors without resorting to full ray-tracing or molecular dynamics simulation. For most MEMS-scale or fiber-based devices, where substrate diameters range from tens to hundreds of micrometers, this condition is easily met for typical PVD throw distances of 5–30 cm. However, for use-cases in which <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M274" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi></math></span> is not small, <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a> must be modified to account for error factors beyond <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M275" display="inline"><mi>O</mi><mfenced separators="|"><mrow><msup><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></mrow></mfenced></math></span>, where we recall <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M276" display="inline"><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi></math></span>. See Supplementary Materials for more details.</p>
<p id="P66">Because the model incorporates surface and grain-boundary scattering through the FS–MS framework, it extends naturally to other metals and alloys used in microelectronic applications. By substituting appropriate material constants—bulk resistivity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M277" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>ρ</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span>, mean free path <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M278" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub></math></span>, grain size <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M279" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, and reflection coefficients <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M280" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M281" display="inline"><mi>P</mi></math></span>—the model can predict electrical performance for Ag, Cu, Al, Ti, Pt, and Ni films. For example, copper and silver, which exhibit higher specular scattering fractions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M282" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>P</mi><mo>≈</mo><mn>0.3</mn><mo>-</mo><mn>0.4</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> and longer mean free paths, are expected to show a weaker thickness dependence of resistivity than gold, while refractory metals such as Ti or Pt may exhibit stronger deviations due to increased grain-boundary scattering and smaller <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M283" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>; <a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Zhang et al. (2011)</a>, <a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Glushko and Dehm (2019)</a>). These variations can be quantified directly through the substitution of material parameters in <a href="#FD31" class="usa-link">Eq. (27)</a>, making the framework compositionally generalizable.</p>
<p id="P67">Specifically, although this study focuses on metallic thin films, specifically thin gold films, the geometric deposition formalism and analytical model for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M284" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub></math></span> remain valid for dielectric, semiconducting, and even non-conductive coatings such as ceramic deposited under anisotropic PVD conditions (<a href="#R38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R38">Tyunkov et al. (2022)</a>; <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">Oks et al. (2017)</a>). For dielectric materials such as SiO<sub>2</sub>, TiO<sub>2</sub>, or Al<sub>2</sub>O<sub>3</sub>, the same angular flux distribution governs local film thickness, though the electrical behavior shifts from resistive to capacitive or insulating. In such cases, <a href="#FD18" class="usa-link">Eq. (16)</a> still provides the spatial dependence of film thickness necessary for modeling dielectric breakdown strength, leakage pathways, or optical interference effects in cylindrical geometries (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">Kameshkov and Gerasimov (2024)</a>). Similarly, when considering layered or composite metallizations such as Ti/Au or Cr/Au stacks, inter-layer diffusion and adhesion layers modify effective scattering parameters, introducing additional interfaces that can be modeled through series or parallel resistance combinations (<a href="#R37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R37">Todeschini et al. (2017)</a>). Thus, the model provides a versatile foundation for expanding beyond pure-metal systems to composite and functional thin films.</p></section><section id="S24"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">5.4.2. Implications for MEMS and Bio-MEMS</h4>
<p id="P68">The predictive relationship between deposition geometry, film thickness, and electrical resistance established in this work holds significant implications for the design of MEMS and bio-MEMS devices that rely on precise conductive pathways. For micro-fabricated sensors, resonators, and micro-actuators, resistance uniformity directly influences signal stability, thermal dissipation, and response time (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>; <a href="#R2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R2">Cheng et al. (2025)</a>). The derived analytical form of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M285" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">cyl</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> enables engineers to pre-compute resistance values for given deposition parameters, minimizing the need for iterative fabrication trials.</p>
<p id="P69">In bio-MEMS and neural interface applications, the use of cylindrical or fiber-based geometries is quite common. For example, these substrates are used in fabricating micro-electrode arrays, catheter-embedded sensors, and neural stimulation leads (<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>; <a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Zaidi et al. (2025)</a>). For such systems, the ability to predict resistive behavior as a function of deposition time and geometry allows for finer control over signal impedance, heating, conductance, and biocompatibility. Because the model is purely geometric in its core form, it can be adapted to biocompatible metals such as platinum, iridium, or titanium nitride (TiN) simply by substituting material constants, facilitating safer and more predictable device performance in vivo.</p>
<p id="P70">Furthermore, the anisotropic nature of PVD described by this framework can be leveraged intentionally for functional design. By tuning the orientation of the substrate, selective metallization can be achieved along exposed cylindrical regions while shadowed areas remain uncoated. This can be taken further, where differing layer ratios with multi-material stack-based deposition can result in differing, predictable, and tunable electrical properties throughout the wire or fiber substrate. In sum, the presented model serves not only as an interpretive tool for existing deposition processes but also as a predictive design framework for next-generation MEMS and bio-MEMS architectures requiring spatially controlled conductive coatings.</p></section></section><section id="S25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">5.5. Limitations and Future Study</h3>
<p id="P71">While the presented framework successfully integrates geometric, transport, and empirical modeling to predict resistive behavior in anisotropically deposited thin films, several simplifying assumptions constrain its quantitative generality. These limitations, in addition to those discussed in <a href="#S13" class="usa-link">Section 5</a>, primarily arise from the idealizations necessary for analytic tractability and the practical constraints of experimental validation.</p>
<p id="P72"><strong>(1) Point-source and Vacuum Idealization:</strong> The deposition model assumes a point-like emission source and negligible gas-phase scattering (free molecular flow assumptions), implying that all particle trajectories follow direct line-of-sight paths. In practice, however, as discussed in <a href="#S19" class="usa-link">section 5.3</a>, thermal and electron-beam evaporators exhibit finite source areas and partially diffusive scattering, especially under weaker vacuum environments (&gt; 10<sup>−5</sup> Torr). This can broaden the angular flux distribution and slightly shift the deposition maximum, leading to underestimation of coating uniformity for short throw distances <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M286" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>5</mn><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><mtext>cm</mtext><mo>)</mo></math></span> or larger substrates <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M287" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0.01</mn><mo>)</mo></math></span> (<a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">Panjan et al. (2020)</a>; <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>). Future refinements may incorporate extended-source geometry and partial-pressure corrections to more accurately model weak or intermediate-vacuum regimes without a free molecular flow assumption.</p>
<p id="P73"><strong>(2) Constant Emission and Material Parameters:</strong> The derived model treats the emission exponent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M288" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span>, sticking probability <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M289" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>, and deposition rate constant <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M290" display="inline"><mi>K</mi></math></span> as temporally invariant. However, as the source temperature, chamber pressure, and deposition rate fluctuate during deposition, these parameters can vary dynamically, altering film growth rate, granular geometry, film percolation, and angular uniformity (<a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">Mattox (2010)</a>; <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">Iriarte et al. (2011)</a>). Similarly, the FS–MS parameters <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M291" display="inline"><mfenced close="" separators="|"><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>b</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>g</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mtext>and</mtext><mspace width="thickmathspace"></mspace><msub><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></math></span> are considered uniform and constant across the film thickness and surface. In reality, grain size and specularity evolve during film coalescence, particularly below 200 nm, introducing local heterogeneity not captured by the static model (<a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">Zhang et al. (2011)</a>; <a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">Glushko and Dehm (2019)</a>).</p>
<p id="P74"><strong>(3) Omission of Thermal, Stress, and Diffusion Effects:</strong> The model developed in this study neglects the influence of non-ambient substrate temperature, interfacial stress, and atomic diffusion on film microstructure. Indeed, these processes affect adhesion, grain growth kinetics, and mechanical stability, which in turn affect electron scattering behavior, leading to deviation of measured resistance from model predictions. Gold-on-glass systems, for instance, exhibit temperature-dependent stress relaxation that can induce cracking or delamination at low thicknesses (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">Hutchinson (1996)</a>; <a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">Leib and Thompson (2010)</a>). Including coupled thermo-mechanical modeling or stress-dependent scattering terms could improve the model’s predictive fidelity for multi-material, multi-layer, or high-temperature depositions.</p>
<p id="P75"><strong>(4) Limited Angular Resolution and Measurement Uncertainty.</strong> Empirical characterization of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M292" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M293" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> is constrained by the precision of cross-sectional imaging and two-point resistance measurements. The curved geometry of wire substrates introduces systematic uncertainties in angular mapping and local thickness determination, especially near the occluded regions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M294" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>≈</mo><mi>π</mi><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>. Measurement artifacts, contact resistance, and film cracking may introduce small but persistent biases that limit direct one-to-one correspondence between simulation and experiment. Incorporating four-point probe testing, in-situ monitoring, or automated angular profilometry could reduce these uncertainties in future work.</p>
<p id="P76"><strong>(5) Lack of Dynamic Growth and Rotation Modeling.</strong> The current model framework assumes static deposition without substrate motion. In practical PVD systems, substrate rotation and planetary tooling are frequently employed to improve uniformity, including the wire substrates tested in this study. These dynamic processes effectively average the angular dependence of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M295" display="inline"><mi>f</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> and longitudinal deposition dropoff, complicating closed-form solutions for analytical models. Extending the model to rotational or time-dependent boundary conditions would allow direct prediction of uniformity and resistance under standard industrial configurations (<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">Reinhold et al. (2000)</a>; <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">Jeong et al. (2023)</a>; <a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">Zaidi et al. (2025)</a>).</p>
<p id="P77">Despite these limitations, the close agreement between theoretical and empirical results across three orders of magnitude in resistance demonstrates that the dominant physical processes, i.e., the anisotropic flux geometry and boundary-limited electron scattering, are accurately captured. The framework thus serves as a reliable, first-principles baseline that can be progressively refined to incorporate dynamic, multi-physical, or stochastic phenomena as required for future MEMS and bio-MEMS design.</p></section></section><section id="S26"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">6. Conclusions</h2>
<p id="P78">This study developed and experimentally validated an analytical framework for predicting thin-film deposition morphology and electrical resistance in anisotropic physical vapor deposition (PVD) on cylindrical substrates. By coupling a derived first-principles geometric deposition model with Fuchs–Sondheimer and Mayadas–Shatzkes electron-scattering corrections, a closed-form resistance expression was derived that accurately reproduced both Monte Carlo simulations and measured resistances across 70–3000 nm gold films. The model captures how geometric parameters—throw distance <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M296" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, curvature <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M297" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>R</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>, and emission collimation <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M298" display="inline"><mo>(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span>—govern spatial variations in film thickness and conductivity, while deviations between prediction and experiment arise primarily from micro-structural and mechanical effects inherent to thin-film growth. This framework generalizes to various materials, multilayer electrical structures, and deposition modalities, providing a practical foundation for predictive control of electrical properties with cylindrical substrates. This work offers a predictive design tool for ensuring consistency and efficacy throughout design, iteration, and fabrication of MEMS and bio-MEMS devices.</p></section><section id="SM1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>

<sup>3</sup><p class="display-inline" id="P87">See Supplementary Materials for more details.</p>
</div>
<div class="fn p" id="FN4">
<sup>4</sup><p class="display-inline" id="P88"><a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a> shows the effect of the emission broadening exponent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M300" display="inline"><mi>n</mi></math></span> discussed here.</p>
</div>
<div class="fn p" id="FN5">
<sup>5</sup><p class="display-inline" id="P89">Though a throw distance of 5 cm was selected based on insights gained from <a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">Juliannisa et al. (2025)</a>, as long as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M301" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>≪</mo><mi>D</mi></math></span> (i.e. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M302" display="inline"><mi>R</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>D</mi><mo>≪</mo><mn>1</mn></math></span>), differences in the values for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M303" display="inline"><mi>D</mi></math></span> makes minimal impact on the final deposition profile</p>
</div>
<div class="fn p" id="FN6">
<sup>6</sup><p class="display-inline" id="P90"><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="M304" display="inline"><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mtext mathvariant="italic">max</mtext></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>θ</mi><mo>)</mo></math></span> is the deposition setpoint for PVD systems, as measured by a piezoelectric quartz crystal microbalance.</p>
</div>
